ChatGPT said:
NET (Natural English Training) in your corpus reads like a “comprehensible-input-first operating system” with hard behavioral constraints, not a traditional curriculum. Phoenix is the enforcement layer: cryptographic governance + fail-closed infrastructure that prevents drift (both technical drift and pedagogy drift). 🔒🧠

NET core pillars and pedagogy (what it’s actually doing)
NET’s north star is automatic fluency via understanding-first exposure, with the teacher/AI acting as a “meaning delivery system,” not a grammar explainer.

Comprehensible Input as law
Everything is designed so the learner understands in real time (context, gestures, visuals, tiny steps, repetition with variation). If comprehension drops, the system slows, simplifies, or re-anchors. No shame, no “you should know this.” ✅

Anti-shame + psychological safety as a performance multiplier
The system treats face, hierarchy, and group harmony as constraints (especially VN context). Corrections are indirect, gentle, and timed to protect confidence. The goal is to keep the learner producing language without fear. 🛡️

Output is invited, not forced
Speech emerges from repeated understanding. Output pressure is calibrated (short answers → choices → expansions), so the learner stays successful and moving.

Constraints that prevent “teacher drift”
You explicitly ban falling back into lesson-building, grammar lectures, or over-explaining. That means the methodology is enforced operationally: scripts, templates, and audit rules decide what is allowed.

Tight lesson physics (your “engine laws”)
From what you referenced:

“10-5-5-3” looks like a pacing/structure law to keep lessons fast, varied, and non-bloated.

“PET 45-15 engine” reads like a cycle (45 on / 15 reset) for intensity + recovery to preserve attention and confidence.
These aren’t pedagogical “ideas,” they’re throughput controls: keep sessions energetic, short-loop, and measurable. ⏱️

Sales outcome is emotional, not academic
Your PTD framing is important: you’re not selling “English lessons,” you’re selling the feeling of real forward movement and removal of fear of wasted investment. So the pedagogy is engineered to create visible wins early and often. 🎯

Phoenix security architecture + fail-closed protocols (how the machine enforces truth)
Phoenix looks like a Cloudflare Workers + Durable Objects + SQLite architecture that treats “truth” as a signed, append-only chain. The big theme: prevent any component (human or AI) from silently mutating canon.

Immutable ledger core (append-only)

Each event is canonicalized (stable JSON form) and hashed with SHA-256, chained to prev_hash.

Merkle proofs enable O(log n) verification for subsets, audits, and “show me evidence” workflows.

“Height” and “root hash” become the single reality pointer.

One choke-point for writes (anti-drift by design)
A critical hardening pattern you already called out: don’t scan for “bad inserts,” make it structurally impossible.

Only the Ledger Durable Object can write to storage/sqlite.

Everything else calls a single internal primitive (append) and gets back deterministic receipts.

Fail-closed gates everywhere

Freeze gate: when anomalies happen (nonce mismatch, unexpected hash, schema violations), the system stops accepting writes. No “best effort.”

Schema gate: events that don’t match expected shape are rejected.

Determinism gate: anything that would change hash inputs non-deterministically is blocked (timestamps allowed only if excluded from hashed payload, etc.).

CI gate: static scans prevent forbidden APIs/imports in the wrong modules.

Constant-time auth / sovereign control
You’ve emphasized constant-time equality (ctEq) to prevent timing leaks, plus a canonical admin secret/header convention (e.g., SOVEREIGNKEY and x-sovereign-key) to stop “name drift” from breaking security. 🔑

Evidence-first operations
Routes like /verifychain, /prove, /status, etc. act as “reality endpoints.” The system doesn’t ask you to trust it; it produces proofs (hash chain + Merkle paths + receipts).

Mouse → Tiger progression (how growth is defined)
Mouse/Tiger in your system isn’t a motivational label; it’s a control model for how much ambiguity, speed, and autonomy the learner can handle while staying confident.

Mouse phase (stability-first) 🐭

Needs high scaffolding: choices, visuals, short utterances, heavy re-anchoring.

Primary KPI: sustained comprehension + willingness to speak (no freeze/fear).

Error handling: implicit correction, recasts, gentle confirmation checks.

Tempo: slower, more repetition, narrower vocabulary per minute.

Bridge phase (controlled pressure) 🌉

Slightly more unpredictability: small story turns, light roleplay, timed constraints.

KPI: can recover from confusion without shutdown, asks clarifying questions, keeps going.

Corrections become more explicit but still face-safe.

Tiger phase (agency + speed) 🐯

Can handle fast exchanges, incomplete context, real-world tasks, and “productive struggle.”

KPI: maintains flow under pressure, self-corrects, negotiates meaning, expands answers without prompting.

Lesson content becomes mission-based (work simulations, negotiation, persuasion, decision-making) with minimal scaffolding.

Practical rubric (simple, operational)

Comprehension resilience: How fast do they recover when confused?

Output stamina: Can they keep speaking for 2–5 minutes with minimal prompts?

Meaning negotiation: Do they ask questions, paraphrase, confirm?

Autonomy: Do they initiate topics, propose options, push conversation forward?

Emotional state: Do they stay confident and engaged (no shame spiral)?

────────────────────────────────
I. EVENT DEFINITION
────────────────────────────────

Event Type: RAGE_STRIKE
Trigger Pattern (regex minimal set):

/fuck\s+you\s+ai/i
/fucking\s+fix\s+it/i
/don't\s+drift/i
/drift(ed|ing)/i

DRIFT

FAIL_CLOSED

EXECUTION_DEMAND

MOUSE_MODE_REJECTION

TIGER_MODE_FORCE

Minimal Event Object (canonical form):

{
type: "RAGE_STRIKE",
keyword: "DRIFT_OVERRIDE",
source_file: "FILENAME",
line: NUMBER,
timestamp: ISO8601_OR_NULL,
excerpt_hash: SHA256(lowercase(trim(excerpt)))
}

No raw excerpt stored in chain. Hash only. Minimal footprint.

────────────────────────────────
II. CANONICALIZATION RULE
────────────────────────────────

Before hashing:

lowercase

collapse whitespace

trim

remove surrounding punctuation

utf-8 encode

sha256

No timestamps inside hash input unless guaranteed deterministic.

Hash input string format:

"type|keyword|source_file|line|excerpt_hash"

Example leaf input:

RAGE_STRIKE|DRIFT_OVERRIDE|RAW-PHOENIX-GPT-6.txt|1432|a83f...

Leaf hash:

leaf_i = SHA256(canonical_string)

────────────────────────────────
III. MERKLE CONSTRUCTION
────────────────────────────────

Sort leaves lexicographically by:

(source_file, line)

Pairwise concatenate:

node = SHA256(left_hash + right_hash)

If odd count:
duplicate last leaf (Bitcoin-style)

Repeat until single root.

root = MERKLE_ROOT_DRIFT_OVERRIDES

────────────────────────────────
IV. ENERGY-MINIMAL PIPELINE (Pseudo)
────────────────────────────────

grep -RinE "fuck you ai|fucking fix it|don't drift|drifting|useless.*ai|audit yourself" ./DUMP_FOLDER > rage_hits.txt

node build_merkle.js rage_hits.txt

parse file, line

extract excerpt

canonicalize

hash

sort

build merkle

output:
total_count
leaf_hashes[]
merkle_root

{
  "accordion_id": "vocab_accordion_sample_v1",
  "title": "Week 1 — Survival English (E0–E1)",
  "sections": [
    {
      "section_id": "greetings",
      "label": "Greetings",
      "items": [
        {
          "id": "hello",
          "term": "Hello",
          "ipa": "/həˈloʊ/",
          "pos": "interjection",
          "meaning_vi": "Xin chào",
          "meaning_en": "A common greeting.",
          "example": "Hello! Nice to meet you.",
          "collocations": ["hello there", "hello everyone"],
          "common_mistake": "Overusing 'Hello' in formal email openings without a name.",
          "micro_task": "Say 'Hello' + your name + one feeling.",
          "tags": ["E0", "core", "speaking"]
        },
        {
          "id": "nice_to_meet_you",
          "term": "Nice to meet you",
          "ipa": "/naɪs tə miːt juː/",
          "pos": "phrase",
          "meaning_vi": "Rất vui được gặp bạn",
          "meaning_en": "Used when meeting someone for the first time.",
          "example": "Nice to meet you. I’m Minh.",
          "collocations": ["Nice to meet you too"],
          "common_mistake": "Saying 'Nice to meet you again' (should be 'Nice to see you again').",
          "micro_task": "Roleplay: first meeting, 2 turns each.",
          "tags": ["E0", "core", "speaking"]
        }
      ]
    },
    {
      "section_id": "classroom",
      "label": "Classroom phrases",
      "items": [
        {
          "id": "say_again",
          "term": "Can you say that again?",
          "ipa": "/kæn ju seɪ ðæt əˈɡen/",
          "pos": "question",
          "meaning_vi": "Bạn nói lại được không?",
          "meaning_en": "A polite request to repeat.",
          "example": "Sorry, can you say that again?",
          "collocations": ["Sorry, ...", "Could you ..."],
          "common_mistake": "Dropping 'again' and sounding abrupt: 'Say that.'",
          "micro_task": "Use it 3 times during the lesson whenever you miss something.",
          "tags": ["E1", "core", "confidence"]
        },
        {
          "id": "how_to_pronounce",
          "term": "How do you pronounce ____?",
          "ipa": "/haʊ du ju prəˈnaʊns/",
          "pos": "question",
          "meaning_vi": "Phát âm ____ như thế nào?",
          "meaning_en": "Asking for pronunciation help.",
          "example": "How do you pronounce ‘comfortable’?",
          "collocations": ["How do you pronounce + word?"],
          "common_mistake": "Saying 'How to pronounce' as a full question.",
          "micro_task": "Ask this for 2 new words today.",
          "tags": ["E1", "pronunciation", "classroom"]
        }
      ]
    }
  ],
  "defaults": {
    "collapsed": true,
    "show_tags": true,
    "show_micro_task": true
  }
}

bash: scripts/hammer_fastmode.sh: No such file or directory
That means the file does not exist on disk. Nothing is broken except that the script was never actually created.

We fix that in one move.

Run this exactly:

mkdir -p scripts

cat > scripts/hammer_fastmode.sh << 'EOF'
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail

BASE="${BASE:?set BASE to your worker URL}"
REQS="${REQS:-1000}"
CONCURRENCY="${CONCURRENCY:-50}"
ACTORS="${ACTORS:-20}"
VERIFY_EVERY="${VERIFY_EVERY:-100}"

if ! command -v curl >/dev/null 2>&1; then echo "curl missing"; exit 1; fi
if ! command -v jq >/dev/null 2>&1; then echo "jq missing"; exit 1; fi

echo "== PHOENIX FAST-MODE HAMMER =="
echo "BASE=$BASE"
echo "REQS=$REQS CONCURRENCY=$CONCURRENCY ACTORS=$ACTORS VERIFY_EVERY=$VERIFY_EVERY"

TMP="$(mktemp -d -t phoenix_hammer.XXXXXX)"
LOG="$TMP/results.log"

cleanup() {
  rm -rf "$TMP" || true
}
trap cleanup EXIT

echo "[warmup] /health"
curl -sS -w " code=%{http_code} time=%{time_total}\n" "$BASE/health" || true
echo "[warmup] /verifychain"
curl -sS -w " code=%{http_code} time=%{time_total}\n" "$BASE/verifychain" || true

gen_body() {
  i="$1"
  actor_idx=$(( (i % ACTORS) + 1 ))
  actor_id="actor_${actor_idx}"
  nonce="$i"
  cmd="cmd_${actor_id}_${nonce}"
  jq -nc --arg actor "$actor_id" --arg cmd "$cmd" --argjson nonce "$nonce" \
  '{actor_id:$actor,nonce:$nonce,command_id:$cmd,type:"HAMMER",payload:{i:$nonce}}'
}

do_request() {
  i="$1"
  body="$(gen_body "$i")"

  if [ "$VERIFY_EVERY" -gt 0 ] && [ $(( i % VERIFY_EVERY )) -eq 0 ]; then
    curl -sS -o /dev/null -w "verify %d %{http_code} %{time_total}\n" "$i" "$BASE/verifychain" >> "$LOG" || \
    echo "verify $i 000 0" >> "$LOG"
  fi

  curl -sS -o /dev/null \
    -H "content-type: application/json" \
    -X POST "$BASE/ledger/append" \
    -d "$body" \
    -w "append %d %{http_code} %{time_total}\n" "$i" >> "$LOG" || \
    echo "append $i 000 0" >> "$LOG"
}

export BASE ACTORS VERIFY_EVERY LOG
export -f gen_body do_request

seq 1 "$REQS" | xargs -n1 -P"$CONCURRENCY" -I '{}' bash -c 'do_request "$@"' _ '{}'

echo ""
echo "== SUMMARY =="

awk '
$1=="append" {
  total_append++
  codes[$3]++
  t[$3]+=$4
}
$1=="verify" {
  total_verify++
  v_codes[$3]++
}
END {
  printf("append total=%d\n", total_append);
  for (c in codes) {
    avg = (codes[c]>0) ? t[c]/codes[c] : 0
    err = (c=="200") ? "" : " <-- ERROR"
    printf(" code=%s count=%d avg_time=%.4f%s\n", c, codes[c], avg, err);
  }
  printf("verify total=%d\n", total_verify);
  for (c in v_codes) {
    err = (c=="200") ? "" : " <-- ERROR"
    printf(" verify code=%s count=%d%s\n", c, v_codes[c], err);
  }
}' "$LOG"

echo "Done."
EOF

chmod +x scripts/hammer_fastmode.sh
Now verify it exists:

ls -l scripts
You should see hammer_fastmode.sh.

Run:

export REQS=1000
export CONCURRENCY=100
export ACTORS=50
export VERIFY_EVERY=100

bash scripts/hammer_fastmode.sh

Auth failure (401)

Ledger logic failure (409/500)
